Title: Gravitational Waves from Black Hole Reheating: The Scalar-Induced Component
Authors: Yann Gouttenoire, Nicholas Leister, Pedro Schwaller
Published: 2026-05-20
Categories: hep-ph, astro-ph.CO, gr-qc

MITP-26-024
Gravitational Waves from Black Hole Reheating
The Scalar-Induced Component
Yann Gouttenoirea,b∗, Nicholas Leister b†, Pedro Schwallerb‡
aInstitut d’Astrophysique de Paris (IAP), CNRS, Sorbonne Universit´ e, FR-75014, France
bPRISMA++ Cluster of Excellence&Mainz Institute for Theoretical Physics, Johannes Gutenberg
University, 55099 Mainz, Germany
Abstract
The reheating of the universe by the evaporation of light primordial black holes
(PBHs) can leave a stochastic gravitational-wave (GW) background in the early
Universe. In the monochromatic limit, their simultaneous evaporation produces
an abrupt matter–to-radiation transition, triggering the so-calledPoltergeistGW
signal, usually predicted to be dominant and observable. We revisit this result by
including the irreducible mass spread implied by gravitational collapse in General
Relativity, whose infrared tail scales asd f PBH/dlnM PBH ∝M 3.78
PBH. We show that
this minimal width smooths reheating enough to suppress the Poltergeist background
by orders of magnitude, down to the level of the scalar-induced GW signal produced
during a generic early matter era, such as one driven by the decay of a heavy relic.
We provide a complete decomposition of the scalar-induced spectrum into eight
production channels and find that none, except the one from PBH formation, reaches
either the ∆N eff bound or the projected sensitivity of future GW observatories.
This reopens regions of ultra-light PBH parameter space previously thought to be
excluded by these constraints.

†nleister@uni-mainz.de
‡pedro.schwaller@uni-mainz.de

1 Introduction and summary of results
Primordial black holes (PBHs), first proposed in Refs. [1–4] and reviewed in Refs. [5–10], provide
a powerful probe of the early Universe. PBHs lighter than roughly 10 9 g evaporate before Big
Bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) [11–18], making them a minimal mechanism for reheating the
Universe [19–24].
Ultra-light PBHs can form from enhanced ultra-slow roll during inflation [25–41], gauge-
field production in axion inflation [42, 43], spherical domains produced during inflation [44–55],
domain-wall annihilation [56–67], cosmic-string collapse [68–72], first-order phase transitions
through late-blooming [73–99], matter compression by bubble walls [100–104], bubble colli-
sions [105–108], scalar condensates [109–112], dissipative dark sectors [113–118], during an early
matter era [119–128], or from primordial magnetic fields [129].
Such PBH scenarios predict signatures accessible to cosmological observations. Hawking
evaporation can produce dark matter [130–147], the baryon asymmetry [19, 130, 148–162], hot
relics [141, 163–171], hot spots [172–178], or high-energy cosmic rays [179–182]. Dark matter
and the baryon asymmetry can also be produced by superradiance [183–189]. Another well-
studied probe of ultra-light PBHs is scalar-induced gravitational waves (SIGWs), where tensor
modeshare sourced at second order in the Newtonian potential,h∝Φ 2 [156, 190–198]. The
first contribution is the SIGW signal produced at PBH formation [199], which has received
considerable attention in the context of the PTA signal [200–206], but which forM PBH <10 9 g
lies above kHz frequencies, cf. Eq. (247), near the upper edge of observable frequencies. The
second contribution comes from SIGWs produced at PBH evaporation, which have garnered
significant interest [24, 207–217], see reviews [218, 219]. The dominant signal is the Poltergeist
mechanism [207, 208], sourced at reheating by the rapid oscillation of Newtonian potential Φ
that have been enhanced during the preceding PBH-dominated epoch. The predicted SIGW
signal has been shown to lie in frequency bands accessible to next-generation gravitational wave
observatories, see e.g. [212]. The Poltergeist signal has also been studied in scenarios beyond
PBH reheating [196, 207, 220–224].
A central but often overlooked input is the PBH mass distribution. Broad mass functions
are already known to reduce the amplitude of SIGW signals [208]. In this work, we emphasize
that an extended mass distribution is not an optional model choice but a generic consequence
of gravitational collapse in general relativity. Near the collapse threshold, the PBH mass at
formationM PBH is related to the initial density contrastδthrough the critical-collapse scaling
discovered byChoptuik[225] and further developed in Refs. [226–228]:
Choptuik ′s law:M PBH ∝(δ−δ c)γM,(1)
whereδ c is a threshold andγ M ≃0.36 is a critical exponent. Inserting this scaling into the stan-
dard statistical prescriptions for the PBH abundance, either Press–Schechter formalism [229] or
peak theory [230], see Fig. 5, yields the PBH mass distribution [40, 231–233]

tuning the source.
Key results.In this work, we include this universal infrared tail in the full SIGW computa-
tion. To remain conservative, we adopt a sharp ultraviolet cutoff, corresponding toc 2 →+∞
(see black dotted line in Fig. 6), which maximizes the resulting signal. Our main findings are:
1.Steepened suppression factor.The subhorizon suppression factorS Φ of the Newto-
nian potential Φ across the PBH-to-radiation transition steepens from the monochromatic
4

scalingS Φ ∝k −1/3 toS Φ ∝k −4/3, see Figs. 10 and 12. This reduces the Poltergeist signal
by orders of magnitude compared to the monochromatic prediction, as shown by the top
and middle panels of Fig. 1.
2.PBH reheating behaves as a generic matter phase.Once the critical-collapse
mass spread is included, the SIGW signal from PBH reheating becomes comparable to
the signal generated during a generic early matter-dominated phase [195], such as one
driven by the decay of a heavy relic. The large parametric enhancement of the monochro-
matic Poltergeist signal is therefore lost, with the separate SIGW contribution from PBH
formation being the only exception, see the middle and bottom panels of Fig. 1.
3.BBN and detector reach are relaxed.The suppression of the Poltergeist signal
relaxes both the BBN bound on ∆N eff and the projected reach of future gravitational-
wave observatories. This reopens regions of the (M PBH, βf) plane previously thought to
be excluded or detectable by these probes, see Fig. 2. Existing studies quoting larger
Poltergeist amplitudes [24, 207–219] effectively rely on idealized mass distributions that
omit the universal infrared tail imposed by critical collapse.
4.Eight SIGW channels.We complete the analysis by computing all scalar-induced con-
tributions to Ω GW, summarized in Tab. 1. These contributions can be comparable to
the reduced Poltergeist component and can even dominate in parts of parameter space,
although they remain below the projected sensitivity of future experiments. Complemen-
tary high-frequency channels from PBH mergers and direct Hawking graviton emission
are summarized here, with details left to the companion paper [234].

Benchmark spectra and parameter scan.In Fig. 1, we show the present-day SIGW
spectrum for the benchmarkM PBH = 104 g andβ f = 6×10 −7, corresponding to⟨η eva⟩/ηeq ≃
225, orN MD ≃9.1, to compare directly with Ref. [208]. The plot includes the eight SIGW
contributions listed in Tab. 1. The poltergeist contribution, Ω (RD2,Polt.)
GW , is the most sensitive to
the rate of the matter-to-radiation transition, see also Fig. 13. The contribution sourced during
PBH domination, Ω(eMD)
GW , is only weakly sensitive to this rate, see Fig. 14, while the remaining
contributions are essentially unaffected.
We compare two ultraviolet prescriptions. The fiducial choice,k UV =k NL, cuts the source
when the PBH density field becomes non-linear and gives the controlled perturbative prediction.
The alternative,k UV =k PBH, extends the integral to the mean PBH separation and should be
interpreted as a conservative upper envelope, see Sec. 4.4 for more details. Once the universal
IR tail in the PBH mass distribution is included, the Poltergeist is strongly suppressed and
becomes comparable to the other SIGW channels rather than parametrically dominant.
The corresponding phenomenology in the (M PBH, βf) plane is shown in Fig. 2. We restrict
to the basic PBH mass window set by the lower bound on the Horizon mass from the maximal
inflation scale (see Sec. A.1) and by the requirement of evaporation before BBN: 1 g≲M PBH ≲
109 g.1 We compute the spectra using the universal Choptuik infrared scaling with a sharp high
mass cutoff shown by the black dotted line in Fig. 6. This maximises the signal: a smoother high
mass tail would broaden evaporation further and additionally suppress the SIGW amplitude.
Our spectra are therefore conservative upper envelopes. For projected GW sensitivities we
use the most optimistic combined envelope of future probes shown in Fig. 24 [236–248]. Only
narrow, highly tuned regions remain potentially observable. In this work, we only focus on
the SIGWs components. In [234], we show that the high-frequency components, from Hawking
gravitons and binary mergers, summarised in Sec. 9, are subdominant, see also [142].
Fig. 2 also displays the main consistency limits. The purple contourk NL < keq marks where
perturbations which enter the horizon at the beginning of the PBH domination becomes non-
linear at the time of evaporation. This matters because both the Poltergeist signal is peaked
atk eq. In principle, beyond this contour the linear SIGW prediction carries large theoretical
uncertainty.
The remaining purple contour,F merged >1, delimits the region in which PBH mergers
become important. Following [249, 250], the binary inspiral time is

therefore be interpreted as a conservative upper envelope above that purple line.
Successive mergers also affect the BBN bound. The solid green contour (without clustering)
in Fig. 2 shows the standard bound neglecting mergers, while the contour (with clustering)
includes heavier merger remnants whose delayed evaporation can occur close to, or after, the
onset of BBN [16].

perturbations sourced by PBH evaporation are therefore washed out by proton diffusion and evade the recently
derived bounds on small-scale baryon inhomogeneities [235].

Outline.The remainder of this work systematically analyses GW signatures from realistic
PBH populations in the early Universe. Sec. 2.1 presents the background dynamics of PBH-
dominated scenarios. Sec. 2.2 introduces the PBH gas formed from super-horizon collapse
and derives the resulting extended mass distribution using Press–Schechter statistics and peak
theory. Sec. 3 derives the transfer functions and suppression factors of the cosmological pertur-
bations that source the GWs. Sec. 4 defines the framework for SIGW production and computes
the signal from PBH evaporation in Sec. 5. Sec. 4.4 addresses the sensitivity of the SIGW signal
to the UV cut-off of the scalar power spectrum. We then turn to complementary sources of
gravitational waves, namely SIGWs sourced during the early matter-dominated phase (Sec. 6),
during the early radiation era (Sec. 7), from direct enhancement of the curvature spectrum at
PBH formation (Sec. 8), and high-frequency GWs from PBH mergers and Hawking emission
(Sec. 9). Finally, we conclude. A list of the symbols used throughout this work is provided in
Tab. 2.

2 Background cosmology of evaporating PBHs
We briefly review the background cosmology relevant for primordial black holes (PBHs). On
sufficiently large scales, the Universe is well described by a spatially flat, homogeneous, and
isotropic Friedmann–Lemaˆ ıtre–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) spacetime. In conformal timeη,
the line element takes the form

The comoving Hubble horizon is then defined as the inverse conformal Hubble parameter,
H−1, which sets the characteristic scale of causal connectivity in comoving coordinates. For
a homogeneous and isotropic Universe filled with a perfect fluid of equation-of-state parameter
ω≡ p/ρ, the Einstein equations reduce to the standard Friedmann and continuity relations,

2.1 From formation to evaporation
Black Hole Properties and Evaporation
Once formed, primordial black holes act as pressureless matter on large scales and can be de-
scribed as a non-relativistic fluid with average energy densityρ PBH. As such, they redshift like
ordinary matter. However, unlike stable cold dark matter, PBHs lose mass through Hawk-
ing evaporation [252], eventually disappearing altogether. Expressed in conformal time, the
associated mass loss is described by the logarithmic rate 2

Cosmological History
We focus on ultra-light PBHs that evaporate prior to Big-Bang Nucleosynthesis and assume a
monochromatic mass function characterized by a single mass scaleMPBH, which we identify with
the average PBH mass at formation. PBHs form during radiation domination when overdensities
re-enter the horizon, with a mass set by the horizon mass at that time and controlled by anO(1)
collapse efficiency factorγ H. Their subsequent evolution is governed by Hawking evaporation.
Consistency with the maximal temperature attained after inflation imposes a lower bound on
the PBH mass, while the requirement that evaporation occur before BBN selects the ultra-light
window relevant for this work,
1 g≲M PBH ≲10 9 g.(13)
If the initial PBH energy fraction at formation,β f, exceeds a critical valueβ c, PBHs come
to dominate the energy density before evaporating, giving rise to an early matter-dominated
epoch4
βf > β c ≃2.5×10 −14γ−1/2

⇒eMD(14)
This phase begins at conformal timeη eq, when the PBH and radiation energy densities become
equal, and ends atη eva, when PBHs evaporate. We show the evolution of relative matter and
3This is an idealized scenario in which mass growth from accretion is neglected. Recent studies [264, 265]
indicate that accretion can lead to substantial PBH mass growth for sufficiently large collapse fractionsγ H. The
two analyses, however, find different critical values,γ H,c = 0.395 and 0.495, at which the absorption rate enters
a runaway regime that formally leads to divergent mass growth. As a result, the precise onset of absorption
dominance remains uncertain. In Sec. 2.2 we findγ H ≃[0.2,4] depending on the width of the primordial power
spectrum and on the time at which we evaluateγ H, see Tab. 5. Unlike the analyses of Refs. [264, 265], which
treat the radiation bath as an external reservoir with fixed temperature, realistic scenarios—particularly those
involving ultra-light PBHs that temporarily dominate the cosmic energy budget—do not admit an infinite heat
source: the radiation component becomes subdominant due to differential redshifting and partial absorption. We
therefore expect absorption effects to modify the initial PBH masses by anO(1) factor, without qualitatively
altering the overall picture, which may be captured by the mappingM PBH,f →M PBH,f +M PBH,abs.
4For the sake of simplicity, we omit in this equation and the ones below the factors involving ratios of relativistic
degrees of freedom, which are reported in App. A.

Characteristic comoving scales
Here, we summarize the physical interpretation of the characteristic scales associated with the
formation, evolution, and evaporation of PBHs. The goal is to provide an intuitive overview
of their meaning and mutual relations, while retaining only the final expressions required for
phenomenological applications. All technical derivations including degree-of-freedom factors
can be found in App. A.

horizon,k f =H f, at the time when a sufficiently large overdensity re-enters the horizon
and collapses to form a PBH. It therefore also represents the comoving size of the collapsing
region itself. This scale is directly tied to the PBH mass through the horizon massM H,f
and the scale factora f ≡a(η f) at formation,

2.2 PBH mass distribution
This subsection summarizes the ingredients of the PBH mass distribution underlying our analy-
sis. A complete derivation, the explicit fitting formula for the geometric factorκdefined below,
and a detailed comparison of analytic approaches are presented in App. B. PBHs are assumed
to form during radiation domination from the collapse of large overdensities as they re-enter the
Hubble horizon [4]. Their abundance is characterized by the energy fractionβ, defined as the
fraction of the total energy density converted into PBHs. To track the distribution over masses
we use the differential quantity
dβ(MPBH)
dlnM PBH
,(22)
which gives how the PBH energy density is distributed over masses at formation. The normalized
PBH mass function is then
ψf(MPBH)≡ 1
βf
dβ(MPBH, Tf)
dlnM PBH
,
Z
dlnM PBH ψf(MPBH) = 1,(23)
whereβ f is the total PBH energy fraction at formation. The PBH mass spectrum is set by the
statistics of the primordial curvature perturbations, encoded inP R(k). Throughout this work
we adopt the log-normal parametrization
PR(k) = AR√
2π∆ exp

−ln2(k/kf)
2∆2

,(24)
peaked at the scalek f with width ∆, and we assume Gaussian statistics. The horizon mass
evaluated when this peak scale crosses the horizon is
Mf ≡M H(H=k f).(25)
Near the collapse threshold, the PBH mass is not fixed only by the horizon mass. Numerical
simulations of gravitational collapse show that, for a one-parameter family of initial data la-
beled by an amplitudep, there exists a critical valuep c separating dispersion from black-hole
formation [225, 226, 231, 266]. Forp > p c and close to threshold, the solution first approaches
a universal self-similar critical solution. This critical solution has one unstable mode. The time
spent near it is therefore controlled by the distance to threshold, and the final black-hole mass
obeys the universal scaling law [225–228]
Choptuik ′s law:M PBH =KM H (δm −δ c)γM.(26)
15

Hereδ m is the smoothed density contrast at horizon crossing,δ c is the collapse threshold,
K ∼4 is a coefficient, andγ M is the critical exponent. The scaling in Eq. (26) relies on three
assumptions: the collapsing region is approximately spherically symmetric, the perturbation is
close to threshold,δ m −δ c ≪δ c, and the equation of state is fixed during collapse. The exponent
γM is universal for a given matter content, whileδc andKdepend on the perturbation profile. For
radiation domination one findsγ M ≃0.36 [231, 232, 266], while typical values of the threshold
lie in the rangeδ c ∈[0.40,0.67] [267, 268]. An important subtlety is that the horizon mass
entering Eq. (26) should be associated with the real-space scale controlling collapse, namely the
radiusr m at which the compaction function is maximal, rather than directly with the Fourier
scalek −1
f [267, 269]. We therefore define
MH ≡M H(rm)≡M H(H= 1/r m).(27)
wherer m is the radius at which the compaction function is maximal. The compaction function
C= 2δM/Rmeasures the excess mass inside a spherical region normalized by its areal radius,
and it is this scale rather than the wavelengthk −1
f that controls the collapse [267, 269–275].
The ratio between the two reference scales is parametrized by [275–278]
κ≡k f rm,(28)
which for the log-normal spectrum of Eq. (24) depends only on the width ∆. An explicit fitting
formula is given in Eq. (294) of App. B, and representative values for the widths used in this
work are reported in Tab. 5. Since PBHs form during radiation domination,M H ∝ H −2, and
therefore
MH(rm) =M H(H=k f /κ) =κ 2Mf .(29)
With these geometric factors in hand, we turn to the statistical computation of the mass fraction.
Two analytic frameworks are commonly used to compute the PBH abundance fromP R(k),
namely the Press–Schechter (PS) formalism [229, 230] and peak theory [279], schematically
compared in Fig. 5. In the PS framework, the PBH abundance is estimated from the volume
fraction of regions in which the smoothed density contrast exceedsδ c, while peak theory instead
counts local maxima above threshold. Despite these different counting prescriptions, both
approaches yield the same infrared tail of the logarithmic mass function [266, 272, 280, 281],
ψf(MPBH)∝M 1+1/γM
PBH .(30)
This scaling refers todβ/dlnM PBH, whereas a mass function defined per unit mass,dβ/dM PBH,
scales instead asM 1/γM
PBH . The ultraviolet part of the distribution, including the precise position
and shape of the high-mass cutoff, is model dependent [40, 231–233]. This behavior is illustrated
in Fig. 6, which showsψ f(MPBH) for several widths ∆ and amplitudesA R of the log-normal
spectrum. To retain only the universal IR behavior and discard the model-dependent UV tail,
we adopt the conservative sharp-cutoff form
Choptuik ′s mass dist:ψ sharp

3 Perturbations in a PBH-dominated universe
3.1 The averaged two-fluid background
Before turning to cosmological perturbations and the generation of gravitational waves from
scalar metric fluctuations, we briefly recall the evolution of the homogeneous background. The
background dynamics are governed by a PBH-induced matter component and a radiation bath,
with energy densitiesρ PBH andρ r, respectively, and scale factora. Following Ref. [208], the
standard fluid equations are supplemented by a source term that accounts for PBH evaporation.
It is convenient to express the background evolution in terms of comoving energy densities. The
resulting system of equations reads

Relaxing the idealized assumption of a monochromatic PBH mass spectrum and instead allowing
for an extended mass distributionψ(M PBH) implies that different PBHs evaporate at different
times, thereby prolonging the reheating transition. To obtain a conservative lower bound on
the duration of this modified reheating period, we model the mass function using Choptuik’s
distribution with a sharp cutoff,ψ f,sharp(MPBH), defined in Eq. 31. The use of a sharp cut-
off further allows us to remain largely model agnostic while capturing the minimal physical
broadening implied by critical collapse. We therefore define averaged background quantities on
causally connected regions of the Universe, taken to be Hubble-sized patches. Concretely, for
any functionF(M PBH) we perform the replacement

3.3 Suppression factor after evaporation
In the presence of linear metric perturbations, the PBH decay rate as measured by comoving
observers, Γ obs(⃗ x), may acquire a dependence on the position⃗ x. Indeed, comoving observers
withx i = const measure a proper conformal timedη 2
c = (1+2Φ)dη 2. Hence, they observe PBH
to decay with a rate Γ obs =dlogM PBH/dηc = Γ(1−Φ) where Γ =dlogM PBH/dη. Additionally,
if the PBH fluid has a non-vanishing velocity with respect to the observer, then the decay
rate needs to be Lorentz transformed as well. However, the effect of PBH decay on linear
perturbations can be simplified by working in the synchronous gauge, where the metric takes
the form [208, 291, 292]

4 The SIGW formalism
4.1 From scalar sources to GWs
At first order in cosmological perturbation theory, scalar, vector, and tensor perturbations
decouple. However, at second order, scalar perturbations act as sources for tensor perturbations,
thereby generating GWs [192–194, 298]. These scalar-induced gravitational waves (SIGWs) arise
inevitably at quadratic order in scalar perturbations, even in the absence of primordial tensor
modes. Solving the equation of motion (EOM) for the tensor modesh ij, in the Newtonian
gauge defined by Eq. (49), yields

4.3 A roadmap of GW sources
We consider a cosmological history in which the Universe passes through four successive epochs:
an epoch of PBH formation (form) around a conformal timeη∼η f (Eq. (17)), an initial
radiation-dominated era forη < η eq (RD1), an early matter-dominated era (eMD) driven by
a gas of PBHs duringη eq < η < η eva, and a final radiation-dominated era following PBH
evaporation forη > η eva (RD2),
form− →RD 1 − →eMD− →RD 2 .(118)
Gravitational waves are sourced at second order in the Newtonian potential Φ. The time
evolution of the Newtonian potential through each cosmological epoch is encoded in the transfer
functionT X(k, η), such that

The adiabatic term is the standard radiation-era SIGW background, and thus has the same
origin as the irreducible component generated after evaporation, Ω (RD2,irred.)
GW in Eq. (125).
The isocurvature term is instead sourced by Poisson fluctuations in the PBH number
density. We therefore call it theuniversalcontribution: its spectral shape is fixed by the
statistics of a population of localized objects, largely independently of their microscopic
nature, whether PBHs, solitons, or axion clumps [216, 308–311].
4.Ω (form)
GW (Sec. 8): SIGWs generated at PBH formation, sourced by the enhanced primor-
dial curvature power spectrum responsible for the collapse into PBHs [199, 312]. These
perturbations source GWs at second order upon horizon re-entry, directly linking the GW
signal to the small-scale primordial power spectrum.
Additional GW sources associated with PBHs (direct Hawking emission, gravitational cluster-
ing, and binary inspirals) are discussed in a companion work [234] and summarized in Sec. 9.
4.4 Ultraviolet cut-offk UV
Our computation of the induced GW spectrum rests on two assumptions, namely that scalar
perturbations remain in the linear regime and that the PBH population can be described as
a pressureless perfect fluid withc 2
s =ω= 0. Both assumptions break down at sufficiently
small scales, which motivates an ultraviolet cut-offk UV in Eq. (119) excluding modes for which
the perturbative treatment loses its validity. We adopt two physically motivated choices, the
non-linear scalek NL marking the breakdown of linear theory and the inter-PBH separationk PBH
marking the breakdown of the fluid description of the PBH gas. The two are used as alternatives
rather than combined:k NL provides a conservative lower estimate of the signal by restricting to
modes for which linear theory is unambiguously valid, whilek PBH provides a conservative upper
bound by extrapolating the linear calculation up to the scale at which the fluid description itself
fails. A third cut-off, associated with dissipative damping of the radiation fluid and denoted
kD, is briefly discussed below for completeness but is not included in the present analysis. The
three scales are summarised in Tab. 4.
Non-linear cut-off.The most conservative choice retains only modes that remain in the
linear regime at the epochηof GW emission, that is,k
(X)
UV =k

and isocurvature initial conditions this gives, respectively,

The non-linear scale sets the maximum wavenumber that can be consistently included within
perturbation theory and therefore controls both the peak frequency and the amplitude of the
GW spectrum. The signal is maximised whenβ f ≫β c, since thenk eq > kNL and the dominant
modes enter the horizon during the eMD phase rather than before it. In that regime, the transfer
functions reduce to their super-horizon plateau valuesT (eMD)
Φ ≃9/10 andT (eMD)
S ≃1/5.
The use ofk NL as the ultraviolet cut-off, set by the strict conditionδ PBH = 1, is a deliberately
conservative choice in light of the lattice simulations of Ref. [313]. Those simulations find that
GW production during an eMD epoch remains efficient up to the light-crossing scale of the
largest halos forming prior to reheating,k cross ≃8k NL, beyond which the signal is suppressed by
the incoherence of emission across the halo volume. In the intermediate rangek NL < k < k cross,
non-linear halo collapse contributes additional power on top of the linear prediction, so that
truncating the integral atk NL underestimates the eMD-era contribution Ω (eMD)
GW . No equivalent
lattice study is currently available for the post-reheating Poltergeist component Ω (Polt.)
GW , which
is sourced during radiation domination by the rapid decay of scalar potentials at the end of the
eMD phase, and we apply the same conservative prescription in that regime.

(X)

is valid only on scales larger than the mean comoving inter-PBH separation at formation, which

smaller than previously estimated in the literature, and any overestimate only strengthens that

Dissipation cut-off.The radiation fluid is not perfect. Photons, neutrinos, and other rela-
tivistic species have finite mean free pathsℓ mfp, and on scales comparable toℓ mfp they diffuse
across overdense regions into underdense ones, erasing the underlying scalar inhomogeneity. In
a radiation-dominated background this process is known as Silk damping [314–317] and is gov-
erned by shear viscosity, with heat conduction subdominant at early times. The net effect on
linear scalar perturbations is an exponential suppression of the Newtonian potential on small
scales [318, 319],
Φ(k, η)≃Φ rad(k, η) exp

radius and the mean free path. Refs. [318, 319] have shown that this scale has important con-
sequences for the standard SIGW background, and analogous modifications are expected for
the Poltergeist mechanism, though a quantitative determination ofk D in the PBH-reheating
scenario lies beyond the scope of the present work.
A second dissipative effect, distinct from the suppression in Eq. (137), acts during the post-
reheating phase. Once Φ resumes oscillating as an acoustic wave in the radiation fluid, the same
viscous transport drains energy from these oscillations at a rateτ −1

of durationτ d(k). The resulting suppression of the low-frequency tail of the GW spectrum is
analogous to the mechanism identified in Ref. [318] and is expected to be subdominant relative
to the cut-off on the scalar source in Eq. (137). We leave both effects for future work.

5 SIGWs following PBH evaporation
In this section we compute the gravitational-wave signal emitted during the radiation era that
follows PBH evaporation (RD2), when the frozen Newtonian potential resumes oscillating. The
signal naturally splits into two contributions, distinguished by when the sourcing scalar modes
crossed the Hubble horizon:
•modes that entered during the preceding eMD phase, remained frozen until evapora-
tion, and are released at reheating — their GW contribution is the Poltergeist signal
Ω(RD2,Polt.)
GW [Φ].
•modes that enter only during RD 2 itself and contribute as in a standard radiation era —
their GW contribution is the irreducible background Ω (RD2,irred.)
GW [Φ].
The two populations are separated by the reheating wavenumberk reh, the comoving Hubble
scale at evaporation:
Φ(k, η) = ΦeMD(k, η) Θ(k−k reh)| {z }
→Ω (RD2,Polt.)
GW [Φ]
+ Φ RD2(k, η) Θ(kreh −k)| {z }
→Ω (RD2,irred.)
GW [Φ]
.(139)
Inserted into the quadratic GW source, the two pieces of Φ generate the two adiabatic con-
tributions indicated under the braces. Adding the isocurvature contribution sourced by the
PBH-number-density fluctuationsS, the total RD 2 SIGW spectrum reads
Ω(RD2)
GW = Ω(RD2,Polt.)
GW [Φ] + Ω(RD2,irred.)
GW [Φ] + Ω(RD2,Polt.)
GW [S],(140)
where [Φ] and [S] label the adiabatic and isocurvature seed channels. The irreducible isocur-
vature contribution vanishes, since post-evaporation modes are insensitive to the PBH Poisson
source, which has decayed by then. Mixed terms arising from cross-correlations between the
Poltergeist and irreducible mode sets are neglected: where their frequency supports overlap,
such terms can at most enhance the total signal by anO(1) factor through constructive inter-
ference, or partially suppress it through destructive interference. We show the representative
benchmark spectra in Fig. 13. The technical derivations are collected in App. F.
5.1 The Poltergeist kernel
The dominant contribution to Eq. (122), denoted Ω (RD2)
GW , is generated during the reheating
stage immediately following PBH evaporation. At this stage, the sudden disappearance of the
PBH-dominated matter component triggers rapid oscillations of the Newtonian potential in
the subsequent RD era (noted RD 2). These oscillations correspond to acoustic waves in the
radiation fluid and act as an efficient source of SIGWs [196]. The sharper the transition between
the PBH-dominated and RD epochs, the stronger the resulting sound waves and the larger the
induced GW signal. In the case of PBH evaporation, the decay rate develops a pole, Γ→ ∞
ast→t eva, leading to an almost instantaneous reheating. This extreme non-adiabaticity gives
rise to a pronounced GW signal, commonly referred to as the Poltergeist mechanism [208–
212, 283], which typically dominates GW production from evaporating PBHs. We now outline
the calculation of the Poltergeist GW spectrum Ω (RD2)
GW . Full details are provided in App. F.
Immediately after PBH evaporation, the Newtonian potential is set by its value at the onset
of oscillations, Φ(k, η osc), given in Eq. (75), and subsequently evolves as

5.2 Resonant and low-frequency contributions

k/keva ≫1 relevant for PBH evaporation, the resulting SIGW spectrum admits a transparent
analytic interpretation that captures its dominant features. Using Eq. (96), the tensor modes

Comparison with numerical results
In Fig. 13, we compare our analytical estimates for the peak amplitude of the SIGW spectrum
with full numerical results for ΩGW,0, finding excellent agreement across the relevant parameter
space for both adiabatic and isocurvature sources. The figure also shows a direct comparison
between the SIGW signal from a monochromatic PBH mass 16 function and our new results for
an extended mass distribution following Choptuik’s critical scaling with a sharp UV cut-off. In
16We also confirm earlier studies on monochromatic PBHs [208] as shown in Fig. 23.
40

both cases, numerical results (dashed) are shown alongside the corresponding analytical approx-
imations (solid). The extended Choptuik mass distribution leads to a substantial suppression
of the GW signal — by several orders of magnitude relative to the monochromatic case —
primarily due to enhanced damping at large wavenumbers. This suppression affects both the
adiabatic and isocurvature contributions, with the latter exhibiting an even stronger reduction.
The reason is that the isocurvature power spectrum peaks at high wavenumbers (k∼k PBH),
where the suppression factor scales asS 4
Φ(k)∼(k PBH/keva)−16/3 (Eq. (83)), compared to the
milder (kPBH/keva)−4/3 (Eq. (85)) for a monochromatic mass function.

The irreducible contribution from inflationary SIGWs
After evaporation, the Universe returns to RD, and scalar perturbations that enter the horizon
during this second RD era, RD 2, inevitably source SIGWs. As discussed in App. 7.2, this
contribution can be estimated using the standard radiation-dominated result for a power-law
primordial curvature spectrum. Since SIGWs are dominantly generated around horizon entry
during radiation domination, only modes entering after the onset of RD 2 contribute to this
irreducible signal. We therefore approximate the present-day contribution by
Ω(RD2)
GW,0 (k)≃ D(T, T 0)Q(n s)A2

whereD(T, T 0) accounts for the standard redshifting to today. This RD2 signal is not diluted by
the preceding eMD and provides an irreducible SIGW background associated with the primordial
scalar power spectrum.

6 SIGWs during PBH domination
In this section we compute the SIGW signal emitted during the PBH-dominated, early matter-
dominated (eMD) era, betweenη eq and the onset of reheating. Throughout this phase the
Newtonian potential Φ does not decay on sub-horizon scales, so the quadratic sourcef X in
Eq. (95) remains active and sources GWs continuously. The total signal,
Ω(eMD,PBH)
GW = Ω(eMD,PBH)
GW [Φ] + Ω(eMD,PBH)
GW [S],(170)
combines two contributions: a continuous build-up of the GW amplitude, proportional to the
scale factora, fed by the constant sub-horizon source deep inside the eMD era, and an overall
transfer factor controlled by the way the eMD phase terminates atη eva. Here [Φ] and [S] label
the adiabatic and isocurvature seeds (cf. Eqs. (64) and (65)).
Notation.Inside this section, the suffix (eMD,PBH) distinguishes PBH-driven reheating
from the instantaneous and gradual reference cases, labelled (eMD,inst.) and (eMD,grad.).
Elsewhere in the paper only PBH reheating is considered, and we drop the qualifier, writing
Ω(eMD)
GW ≡Ω (eMD,PBH)
GW .(171)
We proceed in three steps. We first isolate the linear-in-agrowth in the idealised limit of a pure
matter era and clarify its physical origin. We then include the finite eMD-to-RD 2 transition
and specialise to PBH-driven reheating, for which the Choptuik mass function produces a
characteristic 4/3 cusp in the decay of Φ. Finally, we compare the resulting spectrum with
the instantaneous and gradual reference cases and fix the ultraviolet cutoff. We show the
representative benchmark spectra in Fig. 3. The technical derivations are collected in App. G.

(Eq. (100)) can then be evaluated in closed form (see App. G) and reads

sustained sub-horizon sourcing is the origin of the amplification factor∝a(η) appearing in
Eq. (178).

6.3 Comparison with instantaneous and gradual reheating
To assess what is specific to PBH reheating, we compare it with two reference scenarios in
which the transition from matter to radiation domination proceeds at extremely different rates,
namely instantaneous reheating, in which the source is switched off abruptly [196], and gradual
reheating driven by perturbative particle decay at a constant rate Γ∼H, for which the source
is suppressed smoothly [192–195, 197, 198, 298]. The three reheating histories are characterised
by the envelope of the Newtonian potential,
FΦ(η) =



which differ in the way the source is switched off. The instantaneous case is a hard step at
η=η eva, the gradual case is a smooth exponential, and PBH reheating is intermediate between
the two limits: the Choptuik mass function produces a continuous but non-analytic 4/3 cusp
atη=η cut (cf. Eq. (197)).
This distinction matters because the ultraviolet behaviour of the GW kernel is controlled
entirely by how the source switches off, that is, by the behaviour ofF Φ near the endpoint of the
48

time integral in Eq. (195). The three reheating histories therefore generate three qualitatively
different ultraviolet kernels. In the regimex≫x eva ≫1, one finds

A sharper endpoint ofF Φ yields a stronger ultraviolet kernel, with PBH reheating closer to the
instantaneous case than to the gradual one, consistent with the rapid termination of the eMD
phase by Hawking evaporation. The infrared regime, by contrast, is insensitive to the endpoint
and gives a common scalingI X ∝x eva sinxin all three cases.
Since none of the kernels in Eqs. (203)–(205) depends on the internal momentau, v, the scalar
convolution factorises as in Eq. (200), and the three GW spectra inherit the same spectral shape

predictions and carrying a parametric dependence onx eva distinct from either. An explicit
numerical comparison is shown in Fig. 14, and full derivations of the kernels and transfer
factors, including the intermediate regimex eva ∼1, are collected in App. G.
6.4 The UV cutoff
For the GW signal Ω (eMD)
GW generated during the eMD phase, we take the ultraviolet cutoff
kUV to be set by the nonlinear scalek adia/iso
NL , defined in Eqs. (129) and (130), beyond which
linear perturbation theory ceases to be valid. This choice is motivated by numerical simulations
of structure growth during an eMD era, which show that GW production is suppressed once
nonlinear halo formation sets in [313].17 In addition, modes withk > k eq are already suppressed
before the onset of eMD due to the behaviour of the transfer functionT (eMD)
X (k) in Eq. (57).
However, in our analytical estimate, in Eq. (192), we have used the transfer functionsT (eMD)
X (k)
in the regime relevant for modes entering the horizon after the beginning of eMD, namelyk≪
keq. To account conservatively for the suppression of earlier-entering modes, we therefore impose
an additional cutoff atk eq by hand [194, 298]. We thus adopt the prescription summarized in
Tab. 4,

7 SIGWs during the early radiation era
In this section we compute the SIGW signals sourced during a radiation-dominated era. Two
channels are active during the first radiation era RD 1, between PBH formation atη f and
matter–radiation equality atη eq, corresponding in the taxonomy of Sec. 4.3 to the adiabatic
(irreducible) and isocurvature (universal) seeds,
Ω(RD1)
GW = Ω (RD1)
GW [Φ] + Ω (RD1)
GW [S]≡Ω (RD1,irred.)
GW + Ω (RD1,univ.)
GW .(209)
In addition, the post-evaporation radiation era RD 2 carries its own irreducible adiabatic con-
tribution, already announced in Sec. 5,
Ω(RD2,irred.)
GW ≡Ω (RD2)
GW [Φ].(210)
The adiabatic components Ω (RD1)
GW [Φ] and Ω (RD2)
GW [Φ] are sourced by the standard primordial
curvature spectrum and are present in any cosmology with a radiation-dominated phase [192,
194, 298]. They share the same RD-era formalism, differing only in the integration window
of the GW kernel and in the post-emission redshift history, and are therefore derived jointly
below. The isocurvature component Ω (RD1)
GW [S] is sourced instead by the Poisson statistics of the
discrete PBH distribution,⟨S(x)S(y)⟩ ≃ ⟨δ PBH(x)δPBH(y)⟩, which seeds the Newtonian potential
at second order in perturbation theory and generates the universal contribution characteristic of
any localized-object population [216, 218, 308–310]. Crucially, none of these three contributions
benefits from the Poltergeist enhancement of Sec. 5, since GW production takes place entirely in
a radiation background. The two RD1 contributions are further diluted by the entropy release at
PBH evaporation, while the RD 2 contribution is not. As we show below, the universal channel
inherits an additional radiation-eraκ −2 suppression of the isocurvature-to-curvature transfer,
fixing the geometric hierarchy Ω(RD1)
GW [S]∝(k eq/kPBH)4, while the two adiabatic channels are set
by the small primordial scalar varianceA2
ζ ∼10 −18. The combined radiation-era signal therefore
lies well below the projected sensitivity of all planned GW observatories. We treat the universal
channel first, then the joint adiabatic derivation for both eras. We show the representative
benchmark spectra in Fig. 15.

8 SIGWs from PBH formation
We now turn to the SIGW component sourced at PBH formation, Ω (form)
GW [Φ]. PBH formation
requires a primordial curvature power spectrumP R(k) that is strongly enhanced around the
formation scalek f defined in Eq. (280). We model this enhancement by the log-normal profile
of Eq. (24), characterized by an amplitudeA R, a width ∆, and a peak positionk f. In the
narrow-width approximation (cf. Eq. (317)), the initial PBH energy fraction is exponentially
sensitive to the smoothed density variance and can be written as

δc ≃0.5 in the numerical analysis. In the plots, however, we do not rely on the asymptotic
expression in Eq. (238). Instead, we use the full numerical Press–Schechter prediction forβ f
derived in App. B, see Eqs. (282) and (298). The same enhanced curvature spectrum that
triggers PBH formation also sources, at second order, an adiabatic scalar-induced gravitational-

9 High-frequency GWs: PBH mergers and Hawking emission
In addition to SIGWs, a gas of PBHs can also generate gravitational waves through mergers
and direct graviton emission. We briefly summarize the relevant production channels here and
refer to the companion paper [234] for a detailed treatment:
1.Isolated Mergers.Because PBHs are randomly distributed in space, they naturally
exhibit local over- and underdensities. When two or three PBHs are sufficiently close,
their mutual gravitational attraction can overcome the Hubble expansion, leading to
the formation of bound systems that decouple from the Hubble flow. These systems
can subsequently inspiral and merge—potentially mediated by the presence of a third
body—thereby emitting gravitational waves.
2.Clusters.Beyond isolated few-body systems, PBHs can form clusters of various sizes
throughout the eMD era. Within these clusters, PBHs virialize, and dynamical inter-
actions—such as hyperbolic encounters—can lead to the formation of bound binaries or
three-body systems. These systems then merge and emit gravitational waves.
3.Hawking evaporation.PBHs emit Hawking radiation with an (approximately) black-
body spectrum (see Sec. 2.1). This emission includes gravitons, with characteristic fre-
quencies set by the initial PBH temperature.
All of these mechanisms produce gravitational waves at very high frequencies, rendering them
inaccessible to current and planned interferometers. In principle, they could contribute to the
effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom,N eff. In [234], these contributions and their
associated spectra are computed in detail, and conservative upper bounds are derived. It is
shown that the high-frequency contributions remain negligible forN eff and therefore do not
modify the newly obtained constraints.
58

10 Conclusion
The main result of this work is that the scalar-induced gravitational-wave (SIGW) signal from
a realistic gas of evaporating ultra-light primordial black holes (PBHs) is orders of magnitude
weaker than the monochromatic Poltergeist amplitudes commonly quoted in the recent litera-
ture. The physical origin of this suppression is the irreducible width of the PBH mass function.
Spherical gravitational collapse in general relativity, through Choptuik’s critical scaling, imposes
the universal infrared behaviour (see Fig. 6)
ψf ∝M 1+1/γM
PBH ≃M 3.78
PBH .
This tail cannot be removed by tuning the primordial curvature spectrum. Once this realistic
mass spread is included, PBHs evaporate over a finite time interval rather than simultaneously.
The matter-to-radiation transition is therefore smoothed, and the post-evaporation suppression
of the Newtonian potential steepens from the monochromatic scalingS Φ ∝k −1/3 toS Φ ∝
k−4/3, see Figs. 10 and 12. As a result, the SIGW spectrum is no longer dominated by the
parametrically enhanced Poltergeist signal. Instead, it becomes qualitatively similar to the
spectrum produced in a generic early matter-dominated era with gradual reheating, such as one
driven by the decay of a heavy long-lived particle. This suppression relaxes the BBN bound
on ∆N eff from PBH-induced SIGWs and reopens regions of the (M PBH, βf) plane that were
previously thought to be excluded or within reach of future GW searches, see Fig. 2.
We have also provided a complete inventory of the SIGW signal in PBH reheating scenarios.
In total, we computed eight distinct contributions to Ω GW, summarized in Tab. 3. These
contributions are generated throughout the cosmological history
form→RD 1 →eMD→RD 2,
where PBH formation is followed by an initial radiation era (RD 1), a PBH-driven early matter
era (eMD), and a final radiation era (RD 2) after PBH evaporation. This decomposition shows
that, once the Poltergeist enhancement is suppressed by the realistic mass distribution, the
remaining SIGW channels become comparable to it and can even dominate in parts of parameter
space, although they remain below the projected reach of future GW observatories. For the
benchmark of Fig. 1,the dominant effect of the realistic mass spread is confined to the post-
evaporation Poltergeist component, Ω (RD2,Polt.)
GW , which is highly sensitive to the duration of
reheating. By contrast, the eMD contribution changes only mildly, while the other SIGW
channels in Tab. 3 are essentially insensitive to the details of the PBH-to-radiation transition.
We also revisited earlier results for the SIGW signal from a monochromatic PBH gas and from
a generic eMD phase with a constant decay rate. We confirm these results and provide new
analytic expressions in Apps. G and I.
The study of the high-frequency contributions from Hawking gravitons and binary mergers,
summarised in Sec. 9, are reported in the companion paper [234] where we show that they do
not impact the constraints in Fig. 2.
Since our result differs from the usual expectation [24, 207–219], we now discuss its robust-
ness. Three independent arguments support this conclusion.
•The IR slope of the PBH mass function is universal.The exponent 1+1/γ M ≃3.78
induced by Choptuik’s critical scaling is fixed by spherical gravitational collapse in general
relativity. It is therefore independent of the PBH formation channel, whether PBHs
originate from inflationary peaks, first-order phase transitions, topological defects, scalar-
field fragmentation, or other mechanisms. Naively, departure from sphericity is expected
to broaden the mass function further and can only strengthen the suppression. While the
critical exponentγ M depends on the equation of stateωat horizon crossing [227], the
high-kscaling of the suppression factor,S Φ ∝k −4/3, does not. Only the overall prefactor
59

ofS Φ depends onγ M, making the suppression weakly sensitive to the equation of state.
Most of this dependence is instead expected to enter through the transfer functions ofS
and Φ, as studied in [283].
•Our prescription is conservative.We have made choices that maximize, rather than
minimize, the SIGW signal. First, we impose a sharp ultraviolet cutoff onψ(M PBH). This
removes the high-mass tail of the PBH distribution. In a realistic mass function, this tail
would make evaporation last longer thanη cut ≃1.26⟨η eva⟩, smooth the 4/3 cusp of the
envelopeF Φ, and further suppress the Poltergeist signal. Second, for both adiabatic and
isocurvature seeds, we consider the two cutoff prescriptions summarized in Tab. 4. Cutting
the GW convolution at the non-linear scalek NL gives a lower estimate, while extending
it up to the fluid scalek PBH gives an upper envelope. Our most aggressive prescription
therefore combines the narrowest mass distribution allowed by general relativity with the
largest physically motivated UV cutoff. It should be interpreted as an upper bound on the
SIGW signal. The middle-left panel of Fig. 2 shows that even with this upper envelope
choice, the SIGW bounds from ∆N eff and the projected reach of GW interferometers
are relaxed wherever the pre-existing BBN bound from energy injection does not already
dominate (green region).
•Agreement with the monochromatic limit.As shown in Fig. 23, in the limitγ M →0
the analytic and numerical pipelines of this work reproduce the monochromatic Poltergeist
amplitudes of Refs. [208, 283], confirming that the orders-of-magnitude reduction we re-
port is driven entirely by the universal IR slope.
A major remaining uncertainty is the contribution from genuinely non-linear halo dynamics
during eMD. Adiabatic lattice simulations [313], whose results are summarised in App. H, find
that the GW signal extends beyond thek NL scale, until the shell-crossing scalek N L ≃8k NL],
while the isocurvature analogue remains unexplored. This makes the latter a natural target for
future numerical work.

B PBH mass distribution following Choptuik’s law
This appendix derives the PBH mass distribution used in Sec. 2.2. We first specify the for-
mation prescription and recall Choptuik scaling. We then introduce the compaction scale and
the geometric factorκ, derive the mass distribution in the Press–Schechter and peak-theory ap-
proaches, summarize the characteristic mass scales in Tab. 5, and finally discuss the narrow-peak
limit.
B.1 PBH formation theory
We consider PBHs formed during radiation domination from the collapse of large superhorizon
density perturbations. After horizon re-entry, a perturbation collapses if the comoving-gauge
density contrast smoothed over a horizon volume,δ m, exceeds a critical thresholdδ c [4]. The
fraction of the total energy density that collapses into PBHs of massM PBH at the epoch when
the horizon mass isM H can be written as [40, 229, 230, 281]

HereP(δ ℓ, MH) is the probability distribution of the linear density contrast smoothed on the

2 δm.(284)
Close to the collapse threshold, the PBH mass is governed by critical collapse. For a one-
parameter family of initial data labeled by an amplitudep, there is a critical valuep c separating
dispersion from black-hole formation. Forp > p c and sufficiently close to threshold, the solution
first approaches a universal self-similar critical solution. Since this critical solution has only one
unstable mode, the final black-hole mass is controlled by the distance to threshold and follows
the Choptuik scaling law [225–228, 231, 232, 266, 280, 294–297]
M(δ m) =KM H (δm −δ c)γM.(285)
The coefficientKdepends on the perturbation profile and is typically of order a few, with
K ≃4 often used as a reference value [231, 266]. The exponentγ M is universal for a fixed
matter content, and for a radiation fluid one findsγ M ≃0.36 [231, 232, 266]. The thresholdδ c
is profile dependent, with numerical studies giving the range 0.40≲δ c ≲0.67 [267, 268]. The
scaling in Eq. (285) assumes approximate spherical symmetry, near-threshold collapse, and a
fixed equation of state during the collapse. The horizon mass at temperatureT H is
MH(TH) = 4πρ

B.2 From the peak scalek f to the collapse scaler m
The Fourier scalek f at which the curvature power spectrum peaks is not, in general, the
real-space scale that controls collapse. The relevant scale is defined by the compaction function,
which measures the excess mass inside a spherical region normalized by its areal radius [267, 269–
275]
C(r, t)≡2 δM(r, t)
Rar(r, t) = 2 MMS(r, t)−M b(r, t)
Rar(r, t) .(291)
HereR ar is the areal radius,M MS is the Misner–Sharp mass, andM b = 4πρ bR3
ar/3 is the
background FRW mass enclosed in the same areal radius. The characteristic radius of the
overdensity is the radiusr m at which the compaction function is maximal,
C′(rm) = 0.(292)
For a curvature power spectrum peaked atk f, we define the geometric factor
κ≡k f rm.(293)
For the log-normal spectrum of Eq. (307),κdepends only on the width ∆ and is well fitted
by [206, 275]
κ(∆)≃1.55 + 1.19
1 + (∆/0.45)2.06 ,0≤∆≤3.(294)
This fit reproduces the tabulated values with a maximum absolute error below 5×10 −3. Rep-
resentative values are given in Tab. 5. Since
Mf ≡M H(H=k f), M H ∝ H −2 (radiation domination),(295)
the horizon mass associated with the compaction scale is
MH(rm)≡M H(H= 1/r m) =M H(H=k f /κ) =κ 2Mf .(296)
Thus the same physical distribution is shifted by a factorκ 2 depending on whether masses are
normalized toM f or toM H(rm). With this convention, the critical-collapse relation becomes
MPBH =Kκ 2Mf (δm −δ c)γM.(297)
B.3 PBH mass distribution
The probability densityP(δ ℓ, MH) entering Eq. (282) can be modeled in two standard ways, the
Press–Schechter formalism and peak theory, which differ in how collapsing regions are counted
but share the same Choptuik induced infrared slope. Combining either prescription with the
critical-collapse relation of Eq. (297) and integrating over horizon masses yields the formation-
time mass distributionψ f(MPBH) used throughout this work.
66

C Evolution ofΦin a radiation-matter universe
In this appendix we derive the evolution of the Newtonian potential Φ in a universe filled with
matter and radiation, and extract the transfer functionsT Φ(κ) andT S(κ) that map adiabatic
and isocurvature initial conditions onto the late-time matter-era amplitude used in the main
text. We first set up the coupled two-fluid perturbation equations in the Newtonian gauge,
casting them as a closed system for Φ and the PBH-radiation isocurvature modeS. We then
solve this system, numerically and through analytic interpolation formulae, and compare our
transfer functions with those of Ref. [283].

D Evolution ofΦduring PBH evaporation
Building on the two-fluid framework of App. C, here we account for the energy exchange that
accompanies PBH decay into radiation, which modifies the perturbation equations relative to
the conserved-fluid case. Working in the synchronous gauge comoving with the PBH component,
we follow the Newtonian potential Φ through PBH domination, evaporation, and reheating, and
obtain an analytic estimate of the suppression factorS Φ(k) entering Eq. (402). We show that Φ
stays on a plateau during PBH domination and decays into oscillations once PBHs evaporate,
and computeS Φ(k) for the Choptuik mass distribution, recovering the monochromatic scaling
of Ref. [208] as a limiting case.
D.1 Perturbation equations
When PBH decays into dark radiation, energy and momentum are exchanged between the two
fluids. At linear order, this exchange modifies the usual conservation equations. Splitting the
time and spatial components of∇ µT µν, one obtains [291]

26References [197, 198] pointed out that the oscillatory behaviour in the GW kernel found in Ref. [195] and
observed in this work (see Figs. 14, 21, and 22) is non-physical and stems from the piecewise definition of
the Green’s function acrossη eva. A more refined treatment replaces the sharp transition by a smooth numerical
interpolation, which does not alter the amplitude or spectral shape qualitatively but averages over the oscillations.
We adopt the simpler piecewise approximation throughout, which suffices for the qualitative estimates presented
here.

F More details on SIGWs following PBH evaporation
This appendix derives the Poltergeist contribution to the SIGW spectrum produced at PBH
evaporation. Our goal is to obtain an explicit expression for the oscillatory kernel and its
time-averaged square entering the GW spectrum.

We cross-checked the above formula for different parameter choices, and found equally close

matic case, the isocurvature-induced gravitational wave signal features a distinct but relatively
flat peak fork≳k eq due to the considerably stronger suppression factor. This makes the
resulting spectrum considerably less sensitive to the ultraviolet cutoff scalek UV.
F.4 Validity of the low-frequency approximation
Unlike the resonant contribution, the low-frequency component cannot be computed fully ana-
lytically in the case of extended PBH mass functions. To see this, we split Eq. (479) into two
integration domains,
Ω(RD2)
GW,LF[X]∝
Z veq
vIR
dv v−10/3+2ns+4n +
Z vUV
veq
dv v−10/3+2ns+4n.(486)
For adiabatic perturbations, the exponent of the integrand is negative for both transfer-function
branches, implying that both integrals are dominated by their lower boundary. However, this
region lies outside the regime of validity of the LF approximation (v≫1), rendering an analytic
estimate unreliable. Consequently, the infrared tail must be evaluated numerically.
For isocurvature perturbations with spectral indexn s = 4, the situation is partially im-
proved. In the rangev∈[0, v eq], the integral is dominated by its upper boundary and remains
within the LF regime, while forv∈[v eq, vUV] it is dominated by the lower boundary and
falls outside it. Nevertheless, as in the monochromatic case [283], the latter contribution is
subdominant.
In light of these considerations, we rely on numerical computations to determine the full
low-frequency tail. Our results show that this contribution is subdominant compared to the
resonant part, and it will therefore be neglected in the remainder of this work.
28Note that the spectral slopes fork≲k eq in Eq. (164) can be recovered by settingn s = 4 in Eq. (163).
Similarly, the prefactor in Eq. (164) arises from the general power-law expression by taking the limitsn s →4,
AR →2/3πandT= 9/10→1/5.

G More details on SIGWs during early matter domination
In addition to GW production from scalar oscillations after reheating (the Poltergeist mecha-
nism), the constant scalar potential Φ during eMD (or slowly decaying during reheating) also
acts as a source (cf. Eq. 95). Hence, we refer to this GW contribution as eMD induced, and
denotes its spectrum by Ω (eMD)
GW . First, we review the case of instantaneous reheating, and
then continue with the extended reheating phase induced by the evaporation of PBHs with the
Choptuik mass distribution.
G.1 Pure MD
An early matter-dominated (eMD) phase, followed by instantaneous reheating atx eva ≡kη eva,
generates a characteristic SIGW signal, which can be derived from the general expression of
Ph(k, η) in Eq. (106). During matter domination, the Newtonian potential remains constant
(Φ′ = 0), which significantly simplifies the source termf X defined in Eq. (95). Deep in the
matter era,x eq ≪x≪x eva, the source term asymptotes to 29

30The physical interpretation of this constant mode has long been debated [330–333]. Ref. [333] clarifies this
issue by focusing on what GW detectors actually measure, namely a time-dependent strain. Since a constant
strain produces no time variation, it yields no observable signal, whether in interferometers, pulsar timing arrays,
or any other detector. In this sense, the constant mode is not a propagating GW and does not contribute
to the observable GW energy density. Therefore, the quantities Ω (pure MD)
GW in Eqs. (174) and (177), as well
as the corresponding results of Ref. [194], are dominated by an unobservable constant contribution and hence
substantially overestimate the physical GW background. The observable signal is instead controlled by the first
time-dependent correction in Eq. (172), implying the replacement
Ω(pure MD)
GW − → 9
x4 Ω(pure MD)
GW ,(493)
in Eqs. (174) and (177) rather than the simple factor-of-2 suppression encoded in Eq. (492). This conclusion
applies when GWs are both produced and observed during MD. The situation is different if the GWs are generated
during MD but observed only after the Universe has re-entered a RD phase. In that case, the constant mode
inherited from the matter era starts oscillating (cf. Eqs. (456) and (457)) and contributes to the kinetic term in
ρGW ∼h ′h′ +∂ ih∂ih, withh ′ ≃kh. As a result, the factor 1/2 in Eq. (492) does not affect the final expressions,
since it cancels out in Ω eMD
GW (η > η eva) in Eqs. (526), (547), and (535). For this reason, we keep this factor only
to facilitate comparison with Ref. [194]: although its interpretation is incorrect for Ω (pure MD)
GW , it leads to the
correct final result for Ω eMD
GW (η > η eva), as discussed in Sec. G.2.

H Non-linearities during eMD
In this appendix we discuss the limitations of the perturbative SIGW calculation during an
eMD era. In eMD, density perturbations grow with the scale factor and can become non-linear
before reheating. The perturbative prediction must therefore be supplemented by a physical
cutoff, which we take to be the scale at which the density contrast reaches order unity. We
compare this prescription with the fully non-linear simulations of Ref. [313], which provide the
best available benchmark for the adiabatic case.
H.1 Adiabatic sources
The simulations show that, once perturbations collapse, the GW signal is no longer described
by the standard second-order SIGW source alone. Two effects become relevant:
1.Non-linear collapse of matter overdensities.During eMD, density perturbations grow with
the scale factor and can reach order unity before reheating. The subsequent collapse, shell
crossing and halo formation source GWs through genuinely non-linear dynamics.
2.Inhomogeneous reheating.The eMD–to–RD transition is spatially modulated by the non-
linear density field. This provides an additional GW source, although simulations indicate
that it is subdominant compared to halo formation in the non-linear regime.
For adiabatic initial conditions, the resulting non-perturbative spectrum is well fitted by [313]
ΩGW(k)≃0.05A 7/4
s
 k
Heq
3/2
,(558)
over the finite range
klow ≲k≲k cross, k low ≃15H eq, k cross ≃ 14H eq
A1/4
s
≃8k NL, k NL ≃ 1.7H eq
A1/4
s
.(559)
The lower scale is set by the latest structures that become non-linear before reheating, while
the upper scale is controlled by the light-crossing time of the largest virialized halos.
Fig. 21 compares the perturbative spectrum with the lattice fit. For gradual reheating, trun-
cating the perturbative integral atk NL reproduces the infrared part of the simulated signal and
connects smoothly to the non-perturbative branch, supporting our fiducial choice. Extending
it to the microscopic UV scale instead overestimates Ω GW by including modes already in the
non-linear regime.
H.2 Isocurvature sources
For isocurvature sources, fully non-linear simulations are not yet available. We nevertheless
apply the same criterion and cut off the perturbative integral when the total density contrast
becomes order unity. The corresponding non-linear scale depends on the PBH mass and is
largest for the lightest allowed PBHs. The maximal spectra obtained in this way are shown in
Fig. 22.
Throughout this work we therefore include only the perturbative eMD signal with a non-
linear cutoff. Additional GWs from halo formation may be present, as suggested by the adiabatic
simulations, but their amplitude is at most marginally within the reach of upcoming experiments
and no corresponding calculation exists for PBH-isocurvature sources. We leave a dedicated
treatment of these non-perturbative contributions to future work.

with non-linear simulations favors the perturbative result with a non-linear cutoff, while extend-
ing the integral to the UV scale gives an unreliably large signal. We therefore use the non-linear
cutoff for the eMD contribution in the parameter scans.
Figure 22: Maximal perturbative eMD signal from Choptuik-smeared PBHs using the non-linear

J GW sensitivities
J.1 GW observatories and BBN bound
In Fig. 24, we show the regions of parameter space constrained by current Pulsar Timing Array
observations (NG15) [334] and by ground-based interferometers from the LIGO–Virgo–KAGRA
(LVK) O3 observing run [244], together with projected sensitivities for the forthcoming LVK
O5 run [245]. We further include sensitivity forecasts for future pulsar timing arrays such
as SKA [240], the proposed lunar laser ranging experiment [248, 335], atom-interferometer
detectors including AION km [243, 336] and AEDGE [242], as well as next-generation space-
and ground-based interferometers. The latter comprise LISA [337, 338], the Einstein Telescope
(ET) [238, 339], Cosmic Explorer (CE) [239],µ-Ares [246], the Big-Bang Observer (BBO) [340],
and THEIA [247]. The Big Bang Nucleosynthesis (BBN) constraint is obtained by imposing
∆Neff ≲0.34, following the procedure detailed in [341].
J.2 Detector sensitivities
Sensitivity estimates are constructed using power-law integrated curves (PLICs), assuming
signal-to-noise ratios and observation times of (SNR = 5, T= 15 yr) for NG15, (SNR =
2, T= 160 days) for LVK O3, (SNR = 10, T= 1 yr) for LVK O5, (SNR = 2, T= 15 yr) for
lunar ranging, (SNR = 10, T= 5 yr) for AION km, (SNR = 10, T= 7 yr) forµ-Ares and
(SNR = 10, T= 10 yr) for SKA, THEIA, LISA, BBO, AEDGE, ET, and CE. The blue violins
show the GW signal detected by Pulsar Timing Arrays (NG15) [342–345].
J.3 Astrophysical foregrounds
Astrophysical foregrounds are also shown. These include the galactic white-dwarf binary back-
ground modeled following [338] (see [346, 347] for alternative treatments), the contribution from
extragalactic supermassive black hole binaries taken from [348], and the background from ex-
tragalactic compact binaries (neutron stars and black holes), modeled as a power law Ω∝f 2/3
calibrated to LVK O3 observations [244].
